Surjectivity of the asymptotic Borel map in Carleman ultraholomorphic classes defined by sequences with shifted moments
Authors: Javier Jiménez-Garrido, Ignacio Miguel-Cantero, Javier Sanz, Gerhard Schindl
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We prove several improved versions of the Borel-Ritt theorem about the surjectivity of the asymptotic Borel mapping in classes of functions with M-uniform asymptotic expansion on an unbounded sector of the Riemann surface of the logarithm. While in previous results the weight sequence M of positive numbers is supposed to be derivation closed, a much weaker condition is shown to be sufficient to obtain the result in the case of Roumieu classes. Regarding Beurling classes, we are able to slightly improve a classical result of J. Schmets and M. Valdivia and reprove a result of A. Debrouwere, both under derivation closedness. Our new condition also allows us to obtain surjectivity results for Beurling classes in suitably small sectors, but the technique is now adapted from a classical procedure already appearing in the work of V. Thilliez, in its turn inspired by that of J. Chaumat and A.-M. Chollet.

Introduction

This paper intends to improve known results on the surjectivity of the asymptotic Borel map in so-called ultraholomorphic Carleman classes in an unbounded sector, both of Roumieu or Beurling type. Such classes of holomorphic functions are defined by restricting the growth of their derivatives, or that of the remainders in their uniform asymptotic expansion, in terms of a given weight sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(M_{p})_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}M=(Mp)p∈N0 of positive real numbers, and the Borel map sends a function into the formal power series providing its expansion. The classical Borel-Ritt-Gevrey theorem of B. Malgrange and J.-P. Ramis [22] solved the problem for Gevrey asymptotics, for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(p!^{\alpha})_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}M=(p!α)p∈N0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\alpha >0$\end{document}α>0. More general situations were covered by J. Schmets and M. Valdivia [24], V. Thilliez [26], A. Debrouwere and the authors [3, 4, 8, 12, 13, 23]. The most satisfactory results are valid for regular weight sequences in the sense of E. M. Dyn’kin [5] (see Sect. 2.1 for the definitions). They determine the length of the interval of surjectivity, consisting of those values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma >0$\end{document}γ>0 such that the Borel map is surjective when considered on classes defined in the unbounded sector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$S_{\gamma}$\end{document}Sγ bisected by the positive real line and with opening πγ in the Riemann surface of the logarithm. Such length is precisely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}γ(M), an index introduced by V. Thilliez [26] for strongly regular sequences and later studied [11] for any weight sequence. The first proof of this fact for Roumieu classes, given in [13] and resting on the result of A. Debrouwere [3] for a halfplane, had an abstract, functional-analytic flavor. Subsequently, and in the same vein as in a previous work of A. Lastra, S. Malek and the third author [15] for strongly regular sequences, the construction of optimal flat functions in sectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$S_{\gamma }$\end{document}Sγ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$0<\gamma <\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}0<γ<γ(M) and for general weight sequences allowed the authors [8] to provide a constructive proof by means of formal Borel- and truncated Laplace-like transforms, defined from suitable kernel functions obtained from those optimal flat functions. Moreover, surjectivity comes with local extension operators, linear and continuous right inverses for the Borel map when acting on suitable Banach spaces within our classes.

The main aim of this paper is to put forward a new condition for weight sequences, much weaker than the condition of derivation closedness included in the definition of regular weight sequences, and still allowing for the obtention of Borel-Ritt theorems in our setting in a constructive way. We say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(M_{p})_{p}$\end{document}M=(Mp)p has shifted moments, (sm) for short, if there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C_{0}>0$\end{document}C0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$H>1$\end{document}H>1 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \log \left (\frac{m_{p+1}}{m_{p}}\right )\le C_{0}H^{p+1},\ \ p\in \{0,1,2, \dots \}, $$\end{document}log(mp+1mp)≤C0Hp+1,p∈{0,1,2,…}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$m_{p}=M_{p+1}/M_{p}$\end{document}mp=Mp+1/Mp. It turns out that, whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$\end{document}γ(M)>0, (sm) amounts to the equivalence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}:=(M_{p+1})_{p}$\end{document}M+1:=(Mp+1)p and the sequence of Stieltjes moments for a kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$e(z)=G(1/z)$\end{document}e(z)=G(1/z) defined from an optimal flat function G in the class defined by M. As we will prove, under this weak condition it is possible to adapt the Borel- and truncated Laplace-transforms in order to make our technique work and obtain local extension operators, and so the surjectivity, of the Borel map for Roumieu classes.

Regarding the Beurling case, A. Debrouwere [3, Th. 7.4] first characterized the surjectivity of the asymptotic Borel map in the right half-plane for regular sequences, and later on he completely solved the problem for non-uniform asymptotics, and provided global extension operators for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma <\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}γ<γ(M) in the case with uniform estimates, see [4]. We will present a different technique in order to treat the problem for classes with uniform estimates, following the same ideas as in the Roumieu case [13], which rest on the use of ramified Borel and Laplace integral transforms. In order to do this, we need to prove Theorem 4.2, which slightly improves both a result of J. Schmets and M. Valdivia [24], Theorem 4.1 in this paper, and the implication \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(i)\Rightarrow (iii)$\end{document}(i)⇒(iii) of the aforementioned result of A. Debrouwere (Theorem 4.4 in this paper). Finally, the new condition (sm) is also valid in order to prove surjectivity for Beurling classes as long as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$0<\gamma <\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}0<γ<γ(M), thanks to a technique of J. Chaumat and A. M. Chollet [2] already applied by V. Thilliez [26, Th. 3.4.1] for strongly regular sequences.

The paper is organized as follows. Section 2 contains the preliminaries, namely: the main information concerning weight sequences, indices or auxiliary functions associated with them, different ultraholomorphic classes and the asymptotic Borel map, and the known result for Roumieu classes by the use of optimal flat functions. In Sect. 3 we obtain the new surjectivity results for Roumieu classes defined by weight sequences satisfying (sm), while Sect. 4 contains the improvements for Beurling classes, according to whether (dc) or (sm) is assumed.

The results included in this paper are part of the Ph.D. dissertation of the second author [16, Ch. 3], defended at the University of Valladolid (Spain) under the advice of the third author.

Preliminaries

Remark 2.1

The form of the inequalities in (sm), (dc) and (mg) admits slight modifications. For example, the constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C_{0}$\end{document}C0 could be omitted in all of them, or one could use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C_{0}H^{p}$\end{document}C0Hp instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C_{0}H^{p+1}$\end{document}C0Hp+1 in (sm) or (dc). The choice of one of these alternative expressions can provide some flexibility in our arguments, and we will use one or another without further comment.

In the classical work of H. Komatsu [14], the properties (lc), (dc) and (mg) are denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(M.1)$\end{document}(M.1), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(M.2)'$\end{document}(M.2)′ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(M.2)$\end{document}(M.2), respectively, while (snq) for M is the same as property \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(M.3)$\end{document}(M.3) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ.

If M is (lc), not only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(m_{p})_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}(mp)p∈N0 but also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(M_{p}^{1/p})_{p\in \mathbb{N}}$\end{document}(Mp1/p)p∈N is nondecreasing, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(M_{p})^{1/p}\leq m_{p-1}$\end{document}(Mp)1/p≤mp−1 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}$\end{document}p∈N; moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\lim _{p\to \infty} (M_{p})^{1/p}= \infty $\end{document}limp→∞(Mp)1/p=∞ if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\lim _{p\to \infty} m_{p}= \infty $\end{document}limp→∞mp=∞. The sequence M is said to be a weight sequence if it satisfies (lc) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$m_{p} \nearrow \infty $\end{document}mp↗∞ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p \to \infty $\end{document}p→∞.

According to E. M. Dyn’kin [5], if M is a weight sequence and satisfies (dc), we say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ is regular. Following V. Thilliez [26], if M satisfies (lc), (mg) and (snq), we say M is strongly regular; in this case M is a weight sequence, and the corresponding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ is regular (since (mg) clearly implies (dc)).

Remark 2.2

All the previously listed properties are preserved when passing from M to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ. However, only (sm), (dc) and (mg) are generally kept when going from M to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\check {{\boldsymbol {M}}}$\end{document}Mˇ. While these statements are well-known for (dc) and (mg), the ones for the, up to our knowledge, new condition (sm) stem from the inequalities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \log \left (\frac{m_{p+1}}{m_{p}}\right )\le \log \left ( \frac{m_{p+1}}{m_{p}}\right )+\log \left (\frac{p+2}{p+1}\right )= \log \left (\frac{\widehat{m}_{p+1}}{\widehat{m}_{p}}\right ) \le \log \left (\frac{m_{p+1}}{m_{p}}\right )+\log (2) $$\end{document}log(mp+1mp)≤log(mp+1mp)+log(p+2p+1)=log(mˆp+1mˆp)≤log(mp+1mp)+log(2) (when applied to M or to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\check {{\boldsymbol {M}}}$\end{document}Mˇ). Similarly, (sm) (like (dc) and (mg)) is kept when going from M to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(p!^{\alpha }M_{p})_{p}$\end{document}(p!αMp)p for any real number α.

Regarding (sm) and (dc) we have the following.

Lemma 2.3

Let M be a sequence such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$a_{0}:=\inf _{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}m_{p}>0$\end{document}a0:=infp∈N0mp>0 (in particular, this holds if M is (lc)). Then, (dc) implies (sm).

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$m_{p}\le C_{0}H^{p+1}$\end{document}mp≤C0Hp+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p+1\le 2^{p}$\end{document}p+1≤2p for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0 and some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C_{0}>0$\end{document}C0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$H>1$\end{document}H>1, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \log \left (\frac{m_{p+1}}{m_{p}}\right )\le \log (m_{p+1})-\log (a_{0}) \le \log (C_{0}/a_{0})+(p+2)\log (H)\le C_{1}H_{1}^{p+1} $$\end{document}log(mp+1mp)≤log(mp+1)−log(a0)≤log(C0/a0)+(p+2)log(H)≤C1H1p+1 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0, with the choices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C_{1}=\log (HC_{0}^{1/2}/a_{0}^{1/2})>0$\end{document}C1=log(HC01/2/a01/2)>0 (note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$a_{0}\le C_{0}H< C_{0}H^{2}$\end{document}a0≤C0H<C0H2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$H_{1}=2$\end{document}H1=2. □

The converse statement in the previous lemma does not hold, as shown by the forthcoming Example 2.5.(iii).

Remark 2.4

Under the same hypothesis of the previous Lemma, one easily checks that the condition ∗\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \exists C_{1}>0,\ H>1\colon \log (m_{p})\le C_{1}H^{p},\quad p\in \mathbb{N}_{0} $$\end{document}∃C1>0,H>1:log(mp)≤C1Hp,p∈N0 implies (sm). Indeed, these two conditions are then equivalent, since (sm) implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \log (m_{p})=\log (m_{0})+\sum _{k=0}^{p-1}\log \left ( \frac{m_{k+1}}{m_{k}}\right ) \le \log (m_{0})+C_{0}\sum _{k=0}^{p-1}H^{k+1}= \log (m_{0})+C_{0}\frac{H^{p+1}-H}{H-1}, $$\end{document}log(mp)=log(m0)+∑k=0p−1log(mk+1mk)≤log(m0)+C0∑k=0p−1Hk+1=log(m0)+C0Hp+1−HH−1, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(*)$\end{document}(∗) is readily obtained. Although the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(*)$\end{document}(∗) is simpler, and easier to check in concrete examples, than (sm), we have preferred to keep the latter as our standard assumption since it enters our arguments directly, see for example the proof of Proposition 3.1.

\setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ is not regular, but they still satisfy (sm). The sequences of the family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{(p^{\tau p^{\sigma}})_{p}\}_{\sigma >1}$\end{document}{(pτpσ)p}σ>1 have appeared as the defining sequences for some generalized ultradifferentiable classes “beyond Gevrey regularity”, deeply studied in a series of papers by S. Pilipović, N. Teofanov and F. Tomić [18–21, 25] and by some of the authors of this paper and their collaborators [7, 9].(iv)The rapidly growing weight sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(q^{p^{p}})_{p}$\end{document}M=(qp)p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$q>1$\end{document}q>1; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$M_{0}:=1$\end{document}M0:=1) are weight sequences which do not satisfy (sm). As it will be seen, they are the only ones in the list to which the results in this paper cannot be applied.

(i)The Gevrey sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(p!^{\alpha})_{p}$\end{document}(p!α)p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\alpha >0$\end{document}α>0) are strongly regular, and their perturbations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(p!^{\alpha}\prod _{j=0}^{p}\log ^{\beta}(e+j))_{p}$\end{document}(p!α∏j=0plogβ(e+j))p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\alpha >0$\end{document}α>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\beta \in \mathbb{R}$\end{document}β∈R) also are after suitably modifying a finite number of their terms, if necessary. They appear all through the study of formal power series solutions to differential and difference equations.

The Gevrey sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(p!^{\alpha})_{p}$\end{document}(p!α)p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\alpha >0$\end{document}α>0) are strongly regular, and their perturbations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(p!^{\alpha}\prod _{j=0}^{p}\log ^{\beta}(e+j))_{p}$\end{document}(p!α∏j=0plogβ(e+j))p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\alpha >0$\end{document}α>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\beta \in \mathbb{R}$\end{document}β∈R) also are after suitably modifying a finite number of their terms, if necessary.
