\setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ is regular, but they are not strongly regular since they do not satisfy (mg). In particular, the q-Gevrey sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(q^{p^{2}})_{p}$\end{document}M=(q2)p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$q>1$\end{document}q>1) appear in the study of q-difference equations.

(iii)The weight sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(q^{p^{\alpha}})_{p}$\end{document}M=(qα)p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$q>1$\end{document}q>1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\alpha >2$\end{document}α>2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(p^{\tau p^{\sigma}})_{p}$\end{document}M=(pτpσ)p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\tau >0$\end{document}τ>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\sigma \ge 2$\end{document}σ≥2) do not satisfy (dc), so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ is not regular, but they still satisfy (sm). The sequences of the family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{(p^{\tau p^{\sigma}})_{p}\}_{\sigma >1}$\end{document}{(pτpσ)p}σ>1 have appeared as the defining sequences for some generalized ultradifferentiable classes “beyond Gevrey regularity”, deeply studied in a series of papers by S. Pilipović, N. Teofanov and F. Tomić [18–21, 25] and by some of the authors of this paper and their collaborators [7, 9].

The weight sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(q^{p^{\alpha}})_{p}$\end{document}M=(qα)p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$q>1$\end{document}q>1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\alpha >2$\end{document}α>2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(p^{\tau p^{\sigma}})_{p}$\end{document}M=(pτpσ)p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\tau >0$\end{document}τ>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\sigma \ge 2$\end{document}σ≥2) do not satisfy (dc), so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ is not regular, but they still satisfy (sm). The sequences of the family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{(p^{\tau p^{\sigma}})_{p}\}_{\sigma >1}$\end{document}{(pτpσ)p}σ>1 have appeared as the defining sequences for some generalized ultradifferentiable classes “beyond Gevrey regularity”, deeply studied in a series of papers by S. Pilipović, N. Teofanov and F. Tomić [18–21, 25] and by some of the authors of this paper and their collaborators [7, 9].

(iv)The rapidly growing weight sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(q^{p^{p}})_{p}$\end{document}M=(qp)p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$q>1$\end{document}q>1; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$M_{0}:=1$\end{document}M0:=1) are weight sequences which do not satisfy (sm). As it will be seen, they are the only ones in the list to which the results in this paper cannot be applied.

The rapidly growing weight sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(q^{p^{p}})_{p}$\end{document}M=(qp)p (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$q>1$\end{document}q>1; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$M_{0}:=1$\end{document}M0:=1) are weight sequences which do not satisfy (sm). As it will be seen, they are the only ones in the list to which the results in this paper cannot be applied.

Following H. Komatsu [14], the relation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}\subset {\boldsymbol{N}}$\end{document}M⊂N between two sequences means that there are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C,h > 0$\end{document}C,h>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$M_{p} \leq Ch^{p}N_{p}$\end{document}Mp≤ChpNp for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p \in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0. We say M and N are equivalent, denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}\approx {\boldsymbol{N}}$\end{document}M≈N, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}\subset {\boldsymbol{N}}$\end{document}M⊂N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{N}}\subset {\boldsymbol{M}}$\end{document}N⊂M; equivalently, there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h_{1},h_{2}>0$\end{document}h1,h2>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h_{1}^{p+1}N_{p}\le M_{p} \leq h_{2}^{p+1}N_{p}$\end{document}h1p+1Np≤Mp≤h2p+1Np for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p \in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0. It is straightforward that (dc) and (mg) are stable under equivalence for general sequences, and the same can be deduced for (snq) for weight sequences by an indirect method, as this condition characterizes the surjectivity of the Borel mapping in Carleman ultradifferentiable classes, by a result of H.-J. Petzsche [17, Cor. 3.2] (see [11, Cor. 3.14] for a direct proof of a more general statement about the stability of (snq)). We prove the result for (sm).

Lemma 2.6

The property (sm) is preserved under equivalence of sequences.

Proof

Suppose M satisfies (sm), and consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{L}}=(L_{p})_{p}$\end{document}L=(Lp)p such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}\approx {\boldsymbol{L}}$\end{document}M≈L. There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C>1$\end{document}C>1 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C^{-p-1}L_{p}\le M_{p}\le C^{p+1}L_{p}$\end{document}C−p−1Lp≤Mp≤Cp+1Lp, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0. Consequently, for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0 one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ l_{p}=\frac{L_{p+1}}{L_{p}}\le \frac{C^{p+2}C^{p+1}M_{p+1}}{M_{p}}=C^{2p+3}m_{p}, \ \ l_{p}\ge \frac{M_{p+1}}{C^{p+2}C^{p+1}M_{p}}=C^{-2p-3}m_{p}. $$\end{document}lp=Lp+1Lp≤Cp+2Cp+1Mp+1Mp=C2p+3mp,lp≥Mp+1Cp+2Cp+1Mp=C−2p−3mp. So, taking into account that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p+1\le 2^{p}$\end{document}p+1≤2p for every p, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document} $$\begin{aligned} \log \left (\frac{l_{p+1}}{l_{p}}\right )&\le \log \left (C^{4p+8} \frac{m_{p+1}}{m_{p}}\right ) \le (4p+8)\log (C)+C_{0}H^{p+1} \\ &\le 4\log (C)+2\log (C)2^{p+1}+C_{0}H^{p+1}\le C_{1}H_{1}^{p+1} \end{aligned}$$ \end{document}log(lp+1lp)≤log(C4p+8mp+1mp)≤(4p+8)log(C)+C0Hp+1≤4log(C)+2log(C)2p+1+C0Hp+1≤C1H1p+1 for the choices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C_{1}=4\log (C)+C_{0}>0$\end{document}C1=4log(C)+C0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$H_{1}=\max \{2,H\}>1$\end{document}H1=max{2,H}>1, so that L also satisfies (sm). □

Given a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}=(M_{p})_{p}$\end{document}M=(Mp)p and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$r>0$\end{document}r>0, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}^{r}:=(M_{p}^{r})_{p}$\end{document}Mr:=(Mpr)p. It is clear that M satisfies (sm) if, and only if, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}^{r}$\end{document}Mr does for some/every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$r>0$\end{document}r>0.

Associated function with a sequence

It is very helpful to consider the associated function to a sequence M given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(t):=\inf _{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}M_{p} t^{p}, \quad t>0;\ h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(0):=0. $$\end{document}hM(t):=infp∈N0Mptp,t>0;hM(0):=0. It is well known that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(t)>0$\end{document}hM(t)>0 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$t>0$\end{document}t>0 if, and only if, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\lim _{p\to \infty}M_{p}^{1/p}=\infty $\end{document}limp→∞Mp1/p=∞, which will be a standard assumption when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}hM enters our considerations. In particular, this condition is satisfied for weight sequences. From [14, Prop. 3.2] we find that, for a weight sequence M, 2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ M_{p}=\sup _{t>0}t^{p} h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(1/t),\quad p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}, $$\end{document}Mp=supt>0tphM(1/t),p∈N0, and it is plain to check that 2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(t)=M_{p+1}t^{p+1} \quad \textrm{for } \frac{1}{m_{p+1}}\le t < \frac{1}{m_{p}},\ p\in \mathbb{N}_{0};\quad h_{{ \boldsymbol{M}}}(t)=1\quad \textrm{for }t\ge \frac{1}{m_{0}}. $$\end{document}hM(t)=Mp+1tp+1for 1mp+1≤t<1mp,p∈N0;hM(t)=1for t≥1m0. Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}hM is nondecreasing and continuous.

Index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}γ(M) for weight sequences

The index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}γ(M), introduced by V. Thilliez [26, Sect. 1.3] for strongly regular sequences M, can be defined for (lc) sequences, and it may be equivalently expressed by different conditions: (i)A sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(c_{p})_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}(cp)p∈N0 is almost increasing if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$a>0$\end{document}a>0 such that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0 we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c_{p}\leq a c_{q} $\end{document}cp≤acq for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$q\geq p$\end{document}q≥p. It is proved in [10, 11] that for any weight sequence M one has 2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})=\sup \{\gamma >0:(m_{p}/(p+1)^{\gamma})_{p \in \mathbb{N}_{0}}\text{ is almost increasing} \}\in [0,\infty ]. $$\end{document}γ(M)=sup{γ>0:(mp/(p+1)γ)p∈N0 is almost increasing}∈[0,∞].(ii)Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\beta >0$\end{document}β>0, we say that m satisfies the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(\gamma _{\beta })$\end{document}(γβ) if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$A>0$\end{document}A>0 such that In [6, 11] it is proved that for a weight sequence M, 2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})=\sup \{\beta >0\colon \,\, {\boldsymbol{m}} \,\, \text{satisfies } (\gamma _{\beta })\,\};\quad \gamma ({ \boldsymbol{M}})>\beta \iff {\boldsymbol{m}}\text{ satisfies }(\gamma _{ \beta }). $$\end{document}γ(M)=sup{β>0:msatisfies (γβ)};γ(M)>β⇔m satisfies (γβ).

(i)A sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(c_{p})_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}(cp)p∈N0 is almost increasing if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$a>0$\end{document}a>0 such that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0 we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c_{p}\leq a c_{q} $\end{document}cp≤acq for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$q\geq p$\end{document}q≥p. It is proved in [10, 11] that for any weight sequence M one has 2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})=\sup \{\gamma >0:(m_{p}/(p+1)^{\gamma})_{p \in \mathbb{N}_{0}}\text{ is almost increasing} \}\in [0,\infty ]. $$\end{document}γ(M)=sup{γ>0:(mp/(p+1)γ)p∈N0 is almost increasing}∈[0,∞].

A sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(c_{p})_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}(cp)p∈N0 is almost increasing if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$a>0$\end{document}a>0 such that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0 we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c_{p}\leq a c_{q} $\end{document}cp≤acq for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$q\geq p$\end{document}q≥p. It is proved in [10, 11] that for any weight sequence M one has 2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})=\sup \{\gamma >0:(m_{p}/(p+1)^{\gamma})_{p \in \mathbb{N}_{0}}\text{ is almost increasing} \}\in [0,\infty ]. $$\end{document}γ(M)=sup{γ>0:(mp/(p+1)γ)p∈N0 is almost increasing}∈[0,∞].

(ii)Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\beta >0$\end{document}β>0, we say that m satisfies the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(\gamma _{\beta })$\end{document}(γβ) if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$A>0$\end{document}A>0 such that In [6, 11] it is proved that for a weight sequence M, 2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})=\sup \{\beta >0\colon \,\, {\boldsymbol{m}} \,\, \text{satisfies } (\gamma _{\beta })\,\};\quad \gamma ({ \boldsymbol{M}})>\beta \iff {\boldsymbol{m}}\text{ satisfies }(\gamma _{ \beta }). $$\end{document}γ(M)=sup{β>0:msatisfies (γβ)};γ(M)>β⇔m satisfies (γβ).

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\beta >0$\end{document}β>0, we say that m satisfies the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(\gamma _{\beta })$\end{document}(γβ) if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$A>0$\end{document}A>0 such that In [6, 11] it is proved that for a weight sequence M, 2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})=\sup \{\beta >0\colon \,\, {\boldsymbol{m}} \,\, \text{satisfies } (\gamma _{\beta })\,\};\quad \gamma ({ \boldsymbol{M}})>\beta \iff {\boldsymbol{m}}\text{ satisfies }(\gamma _{ \beta }). $$\end{document}γ(M)=sup{β>0:msatisfies (γβ)};γ(M)>β⇔m satisfies (γβ).

If we observe that the condition (snq) for M is precisely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(\gamma _{1})$\end{document}(γ1) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{m}}}$\end{document}mˆ, the sequence of quotients for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ, and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma (\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}})=\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})+1$\end{document}γ(Mˆ)=γ(M)+1 (this is clear from (2.3)), we deduce from the second statement in (2.4) that M satisfies (snq) if, and only if, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$\end{document}γ(M)>0.

Let M and L be sequences, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{m}}=(m_{p})_{p}$\end{document}m=(mp)p and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{\ell }}=(\ell _{p})_{p}$\end{document}ℓ=(ℓp)p their respective sequences of quotients. We write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{m}}\simeq {\boldsymbol{\ell }}$\end{document}m≃ℓ whenever there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c>0$\end{document}c>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c^{-1}\ell _{p}\le m_{p}\le c\ell _{p}$\end{document}c−1ℓp≤mp≤cℓp, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0. It is clear that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{m}}\simeq {\boldsymbol{\ell }}$\end{document}m≃ℓ implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}\approx \mathbb{L}$\end{document}M≈L.

We recall the following result for later use.

Lemma 2.7

([11], Remark 3.15)

For an arbitrary sequence M such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>1$\end{document}γ(M)>1, there exists a weight sequence L such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{m}}\simeq \widehat{\boldsymbol{\ell}}$\end{document}m≃ℓˆ, and so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{L}}}\approx {\boldsymbol{M}}$\end{document}Lˆ≈M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma (\widehat{{\boldsymbol{L}}})=\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}γ(Lˆ)=γ(M).

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\alpha >0$\end{document}α>0 we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{\Gamma }}_{\alpha }:=(\Gamma (1+\alpha p))_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}Γα:=(Γ(1+αp))p∈N0, where Γ is Euler’s Gamma function; its sequence of quotients will be denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\overline{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\alpha }$\end{document}g‾α. It is well known that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{\Gamma }}_{\alpha }\approx (p!^{\alpha })_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}Γα≈(p!α)p∈N0, and a straightforward verification shows that for any sequence M and for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\alpha >0$\end{document}α>0 one has 2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document} $$\begin{aligned} \gamma ((p!^{\alpha }M_{p})_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}})&=\gamma ((\Gamma (1+ \alpha p)M_{p})_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}})= \gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})+ \alpha , \end{aligned}$$ \end{document}γ((p!αMp)p∈N0)=γ((Γ(1+αp)Mp)p∈N0)=γ(M)+α,2.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document} $$\begin{aligned} \gamma ((M_{p}/p!^{\alpha })_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}})&=\gamma (M_{p}/( \Gamma (1+\alpha p))_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}})= \gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})- \alpha . \end{aligned}$$ \end{document}γ((Mp/p!α)p∈N0)=γ(Mp/(Γ(1+αp))p∈N0)=γ(M)−α.

Asymptotic expansions, ultraholomorphic classes and the asymptotic Borel map

In what follows \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{R}$\end{document}R stands for the Riemann surface of the logarithm, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]$\end{document}C[[z]] is the space of formal power series in z with complex coefficients.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma >0$\end{document}γ>0, we consider unbounded sectors bisected by direction 0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ S_{\gamma}:=\{z\in \mathcal{R}:|\mathrm{arg}(z)|< \frac{\gamma \,\pi}{2} \} $$\end{document}Sγ:={z∈R:|arg(z)|<γπ2} or, in general, unbounded sectors with bisecting direction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$d\in \mathbb{R}$\end{document}d∈R and opening \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma \,\pi $\end{document}γπ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ S(d,\gamma ):=\{z\in \mathcal{R}:|\mathrm{arg}(z)-d|< \frac{\gamma \,\pi}{2}\}. $$\end{document}S(d,γ):={z∈R:|arg(z)−d|<γπ2}.

A sector T is said to be a proper subsector of a sector S if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\overline{T}\subset S$\end{document}T‾⊂S (where the closure of T is taken in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{R}$\end{document}R, and so the vertex of the sector is not under consideration).

In this paragraph S is an unbounded sector and M a sequence. We start by recalling the concept of uniform asymptotic expansion.

We say a holomorphic function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f\colon S\to \mathbb{C}$\end{document}f:S→C admits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{f}=\sum _{n\ge 0}a_{n}z^{n}\in \mathbb{C}[[z]]$\end{document}fˆ=∑n≥0anzn∈C[[z]] as its uniform M-asymptotic expansion in S (of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$1/h$\end{document}1/h for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h>0$\end{document}h>0) if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C>0$\end{document}C>0 such that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0, one has 2.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left |f(z)-\sum _{n=0}^{p-1}a_{n}z^{n} \right |\le Ch^{p}M_{p}|z|^{p}, \qquad z\in S. $$\end{document}|f(z)−∑n=0p−1anzn|≤ChpMp|z|p,z∈S. In this case we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f\sim _{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}^{u}\widehat{f}$\end{document}f∼fˆM,hu in S, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(S)$\end{document}A˜M,hu(S) denotes the space of functions admitting uniform M-asymptotic expansion of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$1/h$\end{document}1/h in S, endowed with the norm 2.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left \|f\right \|_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h,\overset{\sim}{u}}:=\sup _{z \in S,p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}} \frac{|f(z)-\sum _{k=0}^{p-1}a_{k}z^{k}|}{h^{p}M_{p}|z|^{p}}, $$\end{document}∥f∥M,h,u∼:=supz∈S,p∈N0|f(z)−∑k=0p−1akzk|hpMp|z|p, which makes it a Banach space. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S)$\end{document}A˜{M}u(S) stands for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(LB)$\end{document}(LB) space of functions admitting a uniform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$\end{document}{M}-asymptotic expansion in S, obtained as the union of the previous classes when h runs over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(0,\infty )$\end{document}(0,∞). When the type needs not be specified, we simply write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f\sim _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}^{u}\widehat{f}$\end{document}f∼fˆ{M}u in S. It is also interesting to consider the space of Beurling-type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{({\boldsymbol{M}})}(S):=\cap _{h>0} \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(S)$\end{document}A˜(M)u(S):=∩A˜M,huh>0(S), which becomes a Fréchet space when endowed with the topology generated by the family of (semi)norms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(\left \|\cdot \right \|_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h,\overset{\sim}{u}})_{h>0}$\end{document}(∥⋅∥M,h,u∼)h>0.

Note that, taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p=0$\end{document}p=0 in (2.7), we deduce that every function in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S)$\end{document}A˜{M}u(S) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{({\boldsymbol{M}})}(S)$\end{document}A˜(M)u(S) is a bounded function.

Finally, we define for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h>0$\end{document}h>0 the class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(S)$\end{document}AM,h(S) consisting of the holomorphic functions f in S such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left \|f\right \|_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}:=\sup _{z\in S,p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{|f^{(p)}(z)|}{h^{p}M_{p}}< \infty . $$\end{document}∥f∥M,h:=supz∈S,p∈N0|f(p)(z)|hpMp<∞. (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(S),\left \|\,\cdot \, \right \|_{{ \boldsymbol{M}},h}$\end{document}AM,h(S),∥⋅∥M,h) is a Banach space, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S):=\cup _{h>0}\mathcal{A}_{{ \boldsymbol{M}},h}(S)$\end{document}A{M}(S):=∪AM,hh>0(S) is called a Carleman-Roumieu ultraholomorphic class in the sector S, whose natural inductive topology makes it an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(LB)$\end{document}(LB) space. On the other hand, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{({\boldsymbol{M}})}(S):=\cap _{h>0}\mathcal{A}_{{ \boldsymbol{M}},h}(S)$\end{document}A(M)(S):=∩AM,hh>0(S) is a Carleman-Beurling ultraholomorphic class in the sector S, whose Fréchet space structure is given by the family of seminorms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(\left \|\cdot \right \|_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h})_{h>0}$\end{document}(∥⋅∥M,h)h>0.

We warn the reader that these notations, while the same as in the papers [8, 13], do not agree with the ones used in [12, 23], where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S)$\end{document}A˜{M}u(S) was denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(S)$\end{document}A˜Mu(S), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(S)$\end{document}AM,h(S) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\check {{\boldsymbol {M}}},h}(S)$\end{document}AMˇ,h(S), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S)$\end{document}A{M}(S) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\check {{\boldsymbol {M}}}}(S)$\end{document}AMˇ(S), respectively.

When a statement is valid for both Roumieu and Beurling classes, we will use the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A[M](S), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A˜[M]u(S) and so on (substituting every square bracket by either of them, curly brackets or parentheses, but the same all through the statement).

If M is (lc), the spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A[M](S) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A˜[M]u(S) are algebras, and if M is (dc) they are stable under taking derivatives. Moreover, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}\approx {\boldsymbol{L}}$\end{document}M≈L the corresponding classes coincide.

Since the derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f\in \mathcal{A}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(S)$\end{document}f∈AM,h(S) are Lipschitz, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0 one may define 2.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ f^{(p)}(0):=\lim _{z\in S,z\to 0 }f^{(p)}(z)\in \mathbb{C}. $$\end{document}f(p)(0):=limz∈S,z→0f(p)(z)∈C.

As a consequence of Taylor’s formula and Cauchy’s integral formula for the derivatives, there is a close relation between Carleman ultraholomorphic classes and the concept of asymptotic expansion (for a similar proof see [1, Prop. 8]).

Proposition 2.8

Let M be a sequence and S be a sector. (i)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f\in \mathcal{A}_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},h}(S)$\end{document}f∈AMˆ,h(S) then f admits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{f}:=\sum _{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{1}{p!}f^{(p)}(0)z^{p}$\end{document}fˆ:=∑p∈N01p!f(p)(0)zp as its uniform M-asymptotic expansion in S of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$1/h$\end{document}1/h, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(f^{(p)}(0))_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}(f(p)(0))p∈N0 is given by (2.9). Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h,\overset{\sim}{u}}\le \|f\|_{ \widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},h}$\end{document}∥f∥M,h,u∼≤∥f∥Mˆ,h, and so the identity map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},h}(S)\hookrightarrow \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(S)$\end{document}AMˆ,h(S)↪A˜M,hu(S) is continuous. Consequently, we also have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}(S)\subseteq \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A[Mˆ](S)⊆A˜[M]u(S) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}(S)\hookrightarrow \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A[Mˆ](S)↪A˜[M]u(S) is continuous.(ii)If S is unbounded and T is a proper subsector of S, then there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c=c(T,S)>0$\end{document}c=c(T,S)>0 such that the restriction to T, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} defined on S and admitting a uniform M-asymptotic expansion in S of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} So, the restriction map from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(S)$\end{document}A˜M,hu(S) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},ch}(T)$\end{document}AMˆ,ch(T) is continuous, and it is also continuous from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A˜[M]u(S) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}(T)$\end{document}A[Mˆ](T).

(i)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f\in \mathcal{A}_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},h}(S)$\end{document}f∈AMˆ,h(S) then f admits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{f}:=\sum _{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{1}{p!}f^{(p)}(0)z^{p}$\end{document}fˆ:=∑p∈N01p!f(p)(0)zp as its uniform M-asymptotic expansion in S of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$1/h$\end{document}1/h, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(f^{(p)}(0))_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}(f(p)(0))p∈N0 is given by (2.9). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f\in \mathcal{A}_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},h}(S)$\end{document}f∈AMˆ,h(S) then f admits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{f}:=\sum _{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{1}{p!}f^{(p)}(0)z^{p}$\end{document}fˆ:=∑p∈N01p!f(p)(0)zp as its uniform M-asymptotic expansion in S of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$1/h$\end{document}1/h, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(f^{(p)}(0))_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}(f(p)(0))p∈N0 is given by (2.9). Consequently, we also have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}(S)\subseteq \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A[Mˆ](S)⊆A˜[M]u(S) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}(S)\hookrightarrow \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A[Mˆ](S)↪A˜[M]u(S) is continuous.

(ii)If S is unbounded and T is a proper subsector of S, then there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c=c(T,S)>0$\end{document}c=c(T,S)>0 such that the restriction to T, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f|_{T}$\end{document}f|T, of functions f defined on S and admitting a uniform M-asymptotic expansion in S of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$1/h>0$\end{document}1/h>0, belongs to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},ch}(T)$\end{document}AMˆ,ch(T), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\|f|_{T}\|_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},ch}\le \|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h, \overset{\sim}{u}}$\end{document}∥f|T∥Mˆ,ch≤∥f∥M,h,u∼. If S is unbounded and T is a proper subsector of S, then there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c=c(T,S)>0$\end{document}c=c(T,S)>0 such that the restriction to T, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f|_{T}$\end{document}f|T, of functions f defined on S and admitting a uniform M-asymptotic expansion in S of type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$1/h>0$\end{document}1/h>0, belongs to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},ch}(T)$\end{document}AMˆ,ch(T), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\|f|_{T}\|_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},ch}\le \|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h, \overset{\sim}{u}}$\end{document}∥f|T∥Mˆ,ch≤∥f∥M,h,u∼.
