The asymptotic Borel map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}$\end{document}B˜ sends a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f\in \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(S)$\end{document}f∈A˜M,hu(S) into its M-asymptotic expansion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{f}\in \mathbb{C}[[z]]_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}$\end{document}fˆ∈C[[z]]M,h. By Proposition 2.8.(i) the asymptotic Borel map may be defined from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A˜[M]u(S) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}(S)$\end{document}A[Mˆ](S) into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}$\end{document}C[[z]][M] (with the aforementioned meaning), and from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},h}(S)$\end{document}AMˆ,h(S) into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}$\end{document}C[[z]]M,h, and it is continuous when considered between the corresponding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(LB)$\end{document}(LB), Fréchet or Banach spaces.

If M is (lc), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}$\end{document}B˜ is a homomorphism of algebras; if M is also (dc), differentiation commutes with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}$\end{document}B˜. Finally, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}\approx {\boldsymbol{L}}$\end{document}M≈L implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}=\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{[{ \boldsymbol{L}}]}$\end{document}C[[z]][M]=C[[z]][L], and the corresponding Borel maps are in all cases identical.

We will focus on the surjectivity of the Borel map in unbounded sectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$S_{\gamma}$\end{document}Sγ bisected by direction 0, as this problem is invariant under rotation. So, we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document} $$\begin{aligned} S_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}:=&\{\gamma >0\colon \quad \widetilde{\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{A}_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}(S_{ \gamma})\longrightarrow \mathbb{C}[[z]]_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]} \text{ is surjective}\} , \\ \widetilde{S}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}:=&\{\gamma >0\colon \quad \widetilde{\mathcal{B}}:\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S_{ \gamma})\longrightarrow \mathbb{C}[[z]]_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]} \text{ is surjective}\}. \end{aligned}$$ \end{document}S[Mˆ]:={γ>0:B˜:A[Mˆ](Sγ)⟶C[[z]][M] is surjective},S˜[M]u:={γ>0:B˜:A˜[M]u(Sγ)⟶C[[z]][M] is surjective}.

The surjectivity intervals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$S_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}$\end{document}S[Mˆ] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{S}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}$\end{document}S˜[M]u are either empty or left-open intervals having 0 as endpoint. By Proposition 2.8, we see that 2.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document} $$\begin{aligned} (\widetilde{S}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]})^{\circ}\subseteq S_{[ \widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}\subseteq \widetilde{S}^{u}_{[{ \boldsymbol{M}}]}, \end{aligned}$$ \end{document}(S˜[M]u)∘⊆S[Mˆ]⊆S˜[M]u, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$I^{\circ}$\end{document}I∘ is the interior of I. The determination of these intervals is closely related to the existence of right inverses for the asymptotic Borel map, i.e., linear and continuous operators T such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\circ T$\end{document}B˜∘T is the identity map on a class of formal power series. They are naturally called extension operators, and they can be global, defined from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}$\end{document}C[[z]][M] onto \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}(S)$\end{document}A˜[M]u(S) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}(S)$\end{document}A[Mˆ](S) (with their respective \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(LB)$\end{document}(LB) or Fréchet space structures), or local, at the level of Banach spaces, defined from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}$\end{document}C[[z]]M,h into some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h'}(S)$\end{document}A˜M,h′u(S) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},h'}(S)$\end{document}AMˆ,h′(S) for suitable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h'$\end{document}h′ depending on h. In this latter case, it is common that a scaling of the type occurs, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h'=ch$\end{document}h′=ch for a universal constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c>0$\end{document}c>0 independent from h.

Optimal flat functions and surjectivity of the Borel map in the Roumieu case and under (dc)

The following result for the Roumieu case, already hinted in the work of V. Thilliez [26, Sect. 3.3] and resting on a result of H.-J. Petzsche [17, Th. 3.5], appeared, in a slightly different form, in [12, Lemma 4.5]. Since the result of Petzsche is equally valid for the Beurling case [17, Th. 3.4], one can state the following.

Lemma 2.9

Let M be a weight sequence. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{S}^{u}_{[{\boldsymbol{M}}]}\neq \emptyset $\end{document}S˜[M]u≠∅, then M satisfies (snq) or, equivalently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}}) >0$\end{document}γ(M)>0.

Regarding the precise determination of the surjectivity intervals, the first seminal results appeared in a work of J. Schmets and M. Valdivia [24], who proved that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (0,\lceil \gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})\rceil -1)\subset S_{[ \widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}, $$\end{document}(0,⌈γ(M)⌉−1)⊂S[Mˆ], where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\lceil x\rceil $\end{document}⌈x⌉ is the least integer greater than or equal to a real number x. Moreover, for such openings surjectivity comes with local extension operators with scaling of the type, and with global extension operators in the Beurling case, while global extension operators in the Roumieu case need the extra condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(\beta _{2})$\end{document}(β2) of H.-J. Petzsche [17]. In the case of strongly regular sequences, V. Thilliez [26] showed that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(0,\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}}))\subset S_{[\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}]}$\end{document}(0,γ(M))⊂S[Mˆ], again with local extension operators with scaling of the type. Several improvements followed in the Roumieu case [3, 12, 13, 23], trying firstly to determine the surjectivity intervals, or at least their length, for (certain classes of) strongly regular sequences, and afterwards trying to weaken the condition of moderate growth. These efforts have led to the following precise statement [13, Th. 3.7] under the condition (dc). It shows that the length of the surjectivity intervals is precisely given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}γ(M).

Theorem 2.10

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ be a regular sequence such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$\end{document}γ(M)>0. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (0,\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}}))\subseteq S_{\{ \widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\}}\subseteq \widetilde{S}^{u}_{\{{ \boldsymbol{M}}\}}\subseteq (0,\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})]. $$\end{document}(0,γ(M))⊆S{Mˆ}⊆S˜{M}u⊆(0,γ(M)].In particular, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})= \infty $\end{document}γ(M)=∞, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$S_{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\}}= \widetilde{S}^{u}_{\{{ \boldsymbol{M}}\}}= (0,\infty )$\end{document}S{Mˆ}=S˜{M}u=(0,∞).

Moreover, it turns out that the surjectivity of the Borel map in Carleman-Roumieu classes is closely related to the existence of optimal flat functions.

Definition 2.11

Let M be a weight sequence, S an unbounded sector bisected by direction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$d=0$\end{document}d=0, i.e., by the positive real line \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(0,+\infty )\subset \mathcal{R}$\end{document}(0,+∞)⊂R. A holomorphic function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$G\colon S\to \mathbb{C}$\end{document}G:S→C is called an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$\end{document}{M}-flat function in S if: (i)There exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$K_{1},K_{2}>0$\end{document}K1,K2>0 such that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$x>0$\end{document}x>0, 2.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ K_{1}h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(K_{2}x)\le G(x). $$\end{document}K1hM(K2x)≤G(x).(ii)There exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$K_{3},K_{4}>0$\end{document}K3,K4>0 such that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$z\in S$\end{document}z∈S, one has 2.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |G(z)|\le K_{3}h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(K_{4}|z|). $$\end{document}|G(z)|≤K3hM(K4|z|).

(i)There exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$K_{1},K_{2}>0$\end{document}K1,K2>0 such that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$x>0$\end{document}x>0, 2.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ K_{1}h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(K_{2}x)\le G(x). $$\end{document}K1hM(K2x)≤G(x).

There exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$K_{1},K_{2}>0$\end{document}K1,K2>0 such that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$x>0$\end{document}x>0, 2.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ K_{1}h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(K_{2}x)\le G(x). $$\end{document}K1hM(K2x)≤G(x).

(ii)There exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$K_{3},K_{4}>0$\end{document}K3,K4>0 such that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$z\in S$\end{document}z∈S, one has 2.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |G(z)|\le K_{3}h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(K_{4}|z|). $$\end{document}|G(z)|≤K3hM(K4|z|).

There exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$K_{3},K_{4}>0$\end{document}K3,K4>0 such that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$z\in S$\end{document}z∈S, one has 2.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |G(z)|\le K_{3}h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(K_{4}|z|). $$\end{document}|G(z)|≤K3hM(K4|z|).

Observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$G(x)>0$\end{document}G(x)>0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$x>0$\end{document}x>0, and so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$G(\overline{z})=\overline{G(z)}$\end{document}G(z‾)=G(z)‾, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$z\in S$\end{document}z∈S. The estimates in (2.13) can be rewritten as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |G(z)|\le K_{3}K_{4}^{p}M_{p}|z|^{p},\qquad p\in \mathbb{N}_{0},\ z \in S, $$\end{document}|G(z)|≤K3K4pMp|z|p,p∈N0,z∈S, what exactly means that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$G\in \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S)$\end{document}G∈A˜{M}u(S) and it is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$\end{document}{M}-flat, i.e., its asymptotic expansion is given by the null series. The inequality imposed in (2.12) makes the function optimal in a sense.

The following result is crucial for our purposes.

Proposition 2.12

([8], Prop. 3.10) Let M be a weight sequence with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$\end{document}γ(M)>0. Then, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$0<\gamma <\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}0<γ<γ(M) there exists an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$\end{document}{M}-flat function in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$S_{\gamma }$\end{document}Sγ.

By means of an optimal flat function, one can obtain local extension operators with scaling of the type for Carleman-Roumieu ultraholomorphic classes defined by regular sequences. We briefly describe the procedure.

If G is an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$\end{document}{M}-flat function in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S)$\end{document}A˜{M}u(S), we define the kernel function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$e\colon S\to \mathbb{C}$\end{document}e:S→C given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ e(z):=G\left (\frac{1}{z}\right ),\quad z\in S. $$\end{document}e(z):=G(1z),z∈S. According to Definition 2.11, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$e(x)>0$\end{document}e(x)>0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$x>0$\end{document}x>0, and there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$K_{1},K_{2},K_{3},K_{4}>0$\end{document}K1,K2,K3,K4>0 such that 2.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ K_{1}h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\left (\frac{K_{2}}{x}\right )\le e(x), \quad x>0, \hspace{1cm} \mathrm{and} \hspace{1cm} |e(z)|\le K_{3}h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\left (\frac{K_{4}}{|z|}\right ), \quad z\in S. $$\end{document}K1hM(K2x)≤e(x),x>0,and|e(z)|≤K3hM(K4|z|),z∈S. For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0 we define the p-th moment of the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$e(z)$\end{document}e(z), given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu (p):=\int _{0}^{\infty }t^{p}e(t)\,dt. $$\end{document}μ(p):=∫0∞tpe(t)dt.

By carefully inspecting the proof of [8, Prop. 3.11], where only one implication was mentioned, one can state the following equivalence.

Proposition 2.13

Suppose M is a weight sequence with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$\end{document}γ(M)>0, and G is an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$\end{document}{M}-flat function in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S)$\end{document}A˜{M}u(S) for some unbounded sector S bisected by the positive real line. Consider the sequence of moments \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\boldsymbol{\mu}:=(\mu (p))_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}μ:=(μ(p))p∈N0 associated with the kernel function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$e(z)=G(1/z)$\end{document}e(z)=G(1/z). Then, M satisfies (dc) if, and only if, M and μ are equivalent.

We can already recall the following main result for regular sequences. The previous proposition provides the key fact for the implication \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(ii)\Rightarrow (iii)$\end{document}(ii)⇒(iii).

Remark 2.15

Theorem 2.14 and Proposition 2.8.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(ii)$\end{document}(ii) together guarantee that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma \in (0,\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}}))$\end{document}γ∈(0,γ(M)) there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$a>0$\end{document}a>0 such that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h>0$\end{document}h>0 there exists a local extension operator from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}$\end{document}C[[z]]M,h into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}},ah}(S_{\gamma })$\end{document}AMˆ,ah(Sγ).

Improved Borel-Ritt-Gevrey theorem in the Roumieu case

In this section we present our first main result, where the condition (dc) is changed into the much weaker condition (sm). First, we characterize this new condition in terms of the equivalence of the sequence of moments for a kernel function generated from an optimal flat function and the sequence M shifted by one. This motivates the name given to the condition (sm).

Proposition 3.1

Suppose M is a weight sequence with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$\end{document}γ(M)>0, and G is an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$\end{document}{M}-flat function in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S)$\end{document}A˜{M}u(S), where S is an unbounded sector bisected by the positive real line. Consider the sequence of moments \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\boldsymbol{\mu}:=(\mu (p))_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}μ:=(μ(p))p∈N0 associated with the kernel function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$e(z)=G(1/z)$\end{document}e(z)=G(1/z). Then, M satisfies (sm) if, and only if, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}:=(M_{p+1})_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}M+1:=(Mp+1)p∈N0 and μ are equivalent.

Proof

Suppose M satisfies (sm). On the one hand, because of the right-hand inequalities in (2.14) and the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}hM, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t^{p}e(t)\le K_{3}K_{4}^{p}M_{p}, \ \ p\in \mathbb{N}_{0},\ t>0. $$\end{document}tpe(t)≤K3K4pMp,p∈N0,t>0. So, we may write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document} $$\begin{aligned} \mu (p)&=\int _{0}^{K_{4}m_{p}} t^{p} e(t)\,dt+\int _{K_{4}m_{p}}^{K_{4}m_{p+1}} t^{p} e(t)\,dt +\int _{K_{4}m_{p+1}}^{\infty }\frac{1}{t^{2}}t^{p+2}e(t) \,dt \\ &\le K_{3} K_{4} m_{p} K_{4}^{p}M_{p}+ K_{3} \int _{K_{4}m_{p}}^{K_{4}m_{p+1}} t^{p} h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\left (\frac{K_{4}}{t}\right )\,dt + K_{3}K_{4}^{p+2}M_{p+2} \frac{1}{K_{4}m_{p+1}} \\ &= 2K_{3} K_{4}^{p+1} M_{p+1} +K_{3}K_{4}^{p+1}M_{p+1}\log \left ( \frac{m_{p+1}}{m_{p}}\right ), \end{aligned}$$ \end{document}μ(p)=∫0K4mptpe(t)dt+∫K4mpK4mp+1tpe(t)dt+∫K4mp+1∞1t2tp+2e(t)dt≤K3K4mpK4pMp+K3∫K4mpK4mp+1tphM(K4t)dt+K3K4p+2Mp+21K4mp+1=2K3K4p+1Mp+1+K3K4p+1Mp+1log(mp+1mp), where in the last equality we have used (2.2). Since there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$H>1$\end{document}H>1 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\log (m_{p+1}/ m_{p})\leq H^{p+1}$\end{document}log(mp+1/mp)≤Hp+1 for every p, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu (p)\le K_{3} K_{4}^{p+1} M_{p+1}(2+H^{p+1}) \leq K_{3}K_{4}(2+H)(K_{4}H)^{p}M_{p+1}, $$\end{document}μ(p)≤K3K4p+1Mp+1(2+Hp+1)≤K3K4(2+H)(K4H)pMp+1, and so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\boldsymbol{\mu}\subset {\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}$\end{document}μ⊂M+1.

On the other hand, by the left-hand inequalities in (2.14), for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0 we may estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu (p)\ge \int _{0}^{s} t^{p} e(t)\,dt \ge K_{1} \int _{0}^{s} t^{p} h_{{ \boldsymbol{M}}}\left (\frac{K_{2}}{t}\right )\,dt\ge K_{1} h_{{ \boldsymbol{M}}}\left (\frac{K_{2}}{s}\right )\frac{s^{p+1}}{p+1}. $$\end{document}μ(p)≥∫0stpe(t)dt≥K1∫0stphM(K2t)dt≥K1hM(K2s)sp+1p+1. Then, by (2.1) we deduce that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu (p)\ge \frac{K_{1}}{p+1} \sup _{s>0}h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\left ( \frac{K_{2}}{s}\right )s^{p+1}= \frac{K_{1}}{p+1} K_{2}^{p+1}M_{p+1} \ge K_{1}K_{2}\left (\frac{K_{2}}{2}\right )^{p}M_{p+1}, $$\end{document}μ(p)≥K1p+1sups>0hM(K2s)sp+1=K1p+1K2p+1Mp+1≥K1K2(K22)pMp+1, and so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\subset \boldsymbol{\mu}$\end{document}M+1⊂μ, as desired.

Conversely, suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}${\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\approx \boldsymbol{\mu}$\end{document}M+1≈μ. In particular, there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$C,h>0$\end{document}C,h>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mu (p)\le Ch^{p}M_{p+1}$\end{document}μ(p)≤ChpMp+1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0. By the left-hand inequalities in (2.14), we may estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document} $$\begin{aligned} \mu (p)&\ge \int _{K_{2}m_{p}}^{K_{2}m_{p+1}} t^{p} e(t)\,dt \ge K_{1} \int _{K_{2}m_{p}}^{K_{2}m_{p+1}} t^{p} h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\left ( \frac{K_{2}}{t}\right )\,dt\\ &=K_{1}K_{2}^{p+1}M_{p+1} \log \left ( \frac{m_{p+1}}{m_{p}}\right ),\ p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}. \end{aligned}$$ \end{document}μ(p)≥∫K2mpK2mp+1tpe(t)dt≥K1∫K2mpK2mp+1tphM(K2t)dt=K1K2p+1Mp+1log(mp+1mp),p∈N0. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ K_{1}K_{2}^{p+1}M_{p+1}\log \left (\frac{m_{p+1}}{m_{p}}\right )\leq Ch^{p}M_{p+1}, \quad p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}, $$\end{document}K1K2p+1Mp+1log(mp+1mp)≤ChpMp+1,p∈N0, and M satisfies (sm). □

Remark 3.2

It should be noted that, despite its appearance, the condition (sm) is the appropriate condition when working with the tools of Borel M-summability, since the shift arises naturally. More precisely, given a kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$e(z)$\end{document}e(z) as before, for a function f belonging to a suitable class we can define the e-Laplace transform and the corresponding e-Gamma moment function by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal{L}_{e} (f) (z)=\int _{0}^{\infty }e(u/z) f(u) du, \qquad m_{e} (p) = \int _{0}^{\infty }u^{p-1} e(u) du,\quad p\geq 1. $$\end{document}Le(f)(z)=∫0∞e(u/z)f(u)du,me(p)=∫0∞up−1e(u)du,p≥1. So we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{L}_{e} (u^{p}) (z)= m_{e}(p+1) z^{p+1}$\end{document}Le(up)(z)=me(p+1)zp+1 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0. Consequently, setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$m_{e}(0)=1$\end{document}me(0)=1, these kind of summability techniques can be applied whenever M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(m_{e}(p))_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}(me(p))p∈N0 are equivalent, that is, if and only if M satisfies (sm), since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mu (p)= m_{e}(p+1)$\end{document}μ(p)=me(p+1) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0. Observe that this same shift occurs for the classical Laplace transform given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal{L}(f) (t)=\int _{0}^{\infty }e^{-tu} f(u) du, $$\end{document}L(f)(t)=∫0∞e−tuf(u)du, for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{L}_{e} (u^{p}) (t)= \Gamma (p+1)/t^{p+1} =p!/t^{p+1}$\end{document}Le(up)(t)=Γ(p+1)/tp+1=p!/tp+1 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}$\end{document}p∈N0.

We can already state the following main result, whose proof is an adaptation of the one for Theorem 2.14. Regrettably, we are not able to deduce \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma \le \gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}γ≤γ(M) from the surjectivity of the Borel map in classes on sectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$S_{\gamma }$\end{document}Sγ under this weaker condition (sm).

Theorem 3.3

Let M be a weight sequence satisfying (sm) and with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$\end{document}γ(M)>0, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma >0$\end{document}γ>0 be given. Then, each of the following statements implies the next one: (i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma <\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}γ<γ(M).(ii)There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c>0$\end{document}c>0 such that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h>0$\end{document}h>0 there exists an extension operator from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}$\end{document}C[[z]]M,h into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},ch}(S_{\gamma })$\end{document}A˜M,chu(Sγ).(iii)The Borel map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\colon \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{ \boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S_{\gamma })\to \mathbb{C}[[z]]_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}} \}}$\end{document}B˜:A˜{M}u(Sγ)→C[[z]]{M} is surjective. In other words, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(0,\gamma ]\subset \widetilde{S}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}^{u}$\end{document}(0,γ]⊂S˜{M}u.(iv)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(0,\gamma )\subset S_{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\}}$\end{document}(0,γ)⊂S{Mˆ}.In particular, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(0,\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}}))\subset S_{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}} \}}\subset \widetilde{S}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}^{u}$\end{document}(0,γ(M))⊂S{Mˆ}⊂S˜{M}u.

(i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma <\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}γ<γ(M).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma <\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})$\end{document}γ<γ(M).

(ii)There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c>0$\end{document}c>0 such that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h>0$\end{document}h>0 there exists an extension operator from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}$\end{document}C[[z]]M,h into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},ch}(S_{\gamma })$\end{document}A˜M,chu(Sγ).

There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$c>0$\end{document}c>0 such that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h>0$\end{document}h>0 there exists an extension operator from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}$\end{document}C[[z]]M,h into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{{\boldsymbol{M}},ch}(S_{\gamma })$\end{document}A˜M,chu(Sγ).

(iii)The Borel map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\colon \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{ \boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S_{\gamma })\to \mathbb{C}[[z]]_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}} \}}$\end{document}B˜:A˜{M}u(Sγ)→C[[z]]{M} is surjective. The Borel map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\colon \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{ \boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S_{\gamma })\to \mathbb{C}[[z]]_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}} \}}$\end{document}B˜:A˜{M}u(Sγ)→C[[z]]{M} is surjective. In other words, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(0,\gamma ]\subset \widetilde{S}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}^{u}$\end{document}(0,γ]⊂S˜{M}u.

(iv)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(0,\gamma )\subset S_{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\}}$\end{document}(0,γ)⊂S{Mˆ}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(0,\gamma )\subset S_{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\}}$\end{document}(0,γ)⊂S{Mˆ}.

Proof

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(i)\Rightarrow (ii)$\end{document}(i)⇒(ii) By Proposition 2.12, valid for any weight sequence M with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$\end{document}γ(M)>0, we can consider an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$\end{document}{M}-flat function G in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(S_{\gamma })$\end{document}A˜{M}u(Sγ). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(\mu (p))_{p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}}$\end{document}(μ(p))p∈N0 be the sequence of moments associated with the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$e(z)=G(1/z)$\end{document}e(z)=G(1/z). Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h>0$\end{document}h>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{f}=\sum _{p=0}^{\infty }a_{p}z^{p}\in \mathbb{C}[[z]]_{{ \boldsymbol{M}},h}$\end{document}fˆ=∑p=0∞apzp∈C[[z]]M,h, by the definition of the norm in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}$\end{document}C[[z]]M,h (see (2.10)), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |a_{p}|\le |\widehat{f}|_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}h^{p}M_{p},\quad p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}. $$\end{document}|ap|≤|fˆ|M,hhpMp,p∈N0. Because of Proposition 3.1, there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$h_{1},h_{2}>0$\end{document}h1,h2>0 such that 3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ h_{1}^{p+1}M_{p+1}\le \mu (p)\le h_{2}^{p+1}M_{p+1},\ \ p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}. $$\end{document}h1p+1Mp+1≤μ(p)≤h2p+1Mp+1,p∈N0. So, we deduce that 3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left |\frac{a_{p+1}}{\mu (p)}\right |\le |\widehat{f}|_{{ \boldsymbol{M}},h}\left (\frac{h}{h_{1}}\right )^{p+1},\quad p\in \mathbb{N}_{0}. $$\end{document}|ap+1μ(p)|≤|fˆ|M,h(hh1)p+1,p∈N0. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(ii)\Rightarrow (iii)$\end{document}(ii)⇒(iii) Immediate for any weight sequence M.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(iii)\Rightarrow (iv)$\end{document}(iii)⇒(iv) It follows from (2.11), again valid for any weight sequence. □

New surjectivity results in the Beurling Case

In this last section we collect some results on surjectivity and existence of right inverses for the asymptotic Borel maps for Beurling ultraholomorphic classes. We split the results into two subsections according to the condition imposed on the sequence, (dc) or (sm).

Continuous right inverses under derivation closedness

A first result on the length of the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$S_{(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}})}$\end{document}S(Mˆ) was already provided by J. Schmets and M. Valdivia [24, Th. 4.4 and 4.6], and it can be rephrased as follows. Note that the target space for the Borel mapping in their results is the sequence space whose elements contain the derivatives of a given function at 0, subject to the natural growth restrictions; we are considering instead the target space consisting of the formal power series of asymptotic expansion, or the formal Taylor series, with a suitable control on their coefficients. Both spaces are naturally isomorphic, and both approaches are clearly equivalent. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\lfloor x\rfloor $\end{document}⌊x⌋ stands for the greatest integer which is less than or equal to the real number x.

Theorem 4.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ be a regular sequence and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$r>0$\end{document}r>0. If there is a global extension operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$U_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}:\mathbb{C}[[z]]_{({\boldsymbol{M}})}\to \mathcal{A}_{(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}})}(S_{r})$\end{document}UM:C[[z]](M)→A(Mˆ)(Sr), then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>\lfloor r\rfloor $\end{document}γ(M)>⌊r⌋.

Later on, and regarding strongly regular sequences, the aforementioned result of V. Thilliez [26, Cor. 3.4.1] showed that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$(0,\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}}))\subset S_{(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}})}$\end{document}(0,γ(M))⊂S(Mˆ), and A. Debrouwere [4, Cor. 1.3] has recently proved that surjectivity comes with global extension operators. It is worth noting that [4, Th. 1.2] gives a complete solution to the Borel-Ritt problem in non-uniform classes (which are not treated in this paper) of Beurling type defined by strongly regular sequences.

Going back to results without assuming the condition (mg), we first mention that the hypotheses in Theorem 4.1 can be improved by using some techniques included in [24], and Proposition 4.3 therein, in the same line of ideas that inspired the proof of a similar statement [12, Theorem 4.14(i)] in the Roumieu case. Note that we will exchange the existence of the extension operator into just the surjectivity of the Borel map, and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{A}_{(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}})}(S_{r})\subset \widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{u}_{({\boldsymbol{M}})}(S_{r})$\end{document}A(Mˆ)(Sr)⊂A˜(M)u(Sr), what again weakens the forthcoming assumption.

Theorem 4.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$\end{document}Mˆ be a regular sequence. We recall the following crucial result.

Proposition 4.3

([24], Prop. 4.3)

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$r\in \mathbb{N}$\end{document}r∈N and M be a weight sequence. Proof of Theorem 4.2

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$r\in (0,1)$\end{document}r∈(0,1), then it suffices to observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\widetilde{S}^{u}_{({\boldsymbol{M}})}$\end{document}S˜(M)u is not empty in order to conclude, by Lemma 2.9, that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0=\lfloor r\rfloor $\end{document}γ(M)>0=⌊r⌋ (note that (dc) has not been used in this case).

Suppose now that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$r\ge 1$\end{document}r≥1 and put \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$r_{0}=\lfloor r\rfloor \in \mathbb{N}$\end{document}r0=⌊r⌋∈N. We will show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\mathcal{B}_{r_{0}}:\mathcal{N}_{r_{0},({\boldsymbol{M}})}([0, \infty ))\longrightarrow \mathbb{C}[[z]]_{(\check {{\boldsymbol {M}}})}$\end{document}Br0:Nr0,(M)([0,∞))⟶C[[z]](Mˇ) is surjective, and then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>r_{0}=\lfloor r\rfloor $\end{document}γ(M)>r0=⌊r⌋ by Proposition 4.3, as desired.

Moreover, all of its derivatives of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$m\le pr_{0}-1$\end{document}m≤pr0−1 at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$t=0$\end{document}t=0 vanish, see the proof of [12, Theorem 4.14(i)].

As p is arbitrary, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$f\in \mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})$\end{document}f∈C∞(R) and, moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ f^{(m)}(0)= \textstyle\begin{cases} (-1)^{pr_{0}}b_{p}&\text{if $m=pr_{0}$ for some $p\ge 1$}, \\ 0&\mathrm{otherwise.} \end{cases} $$\end{document}f(m)(0)={(−1)pr0bpif m=pr0 for some p≥1,0otherwise.
